Reasoning about Promises in Weak Memory Models with Event Structures (Extended Version) † † thanks: Wehrheim and Bargmann are supported by DFG-WE2290/14-1. Dongol is supported by EPSRC grants EP/V038915/1, EP/R032556/1, EP/R025134/2, VeTSS and ARC Discovery Grant DP190102142.
Heike Wehrheim, Lara Bargmann, Brijesh Dongol

Abstract
Modern processors such as ARMv8 and RISC-V allow executions in which independent instructions within a process may be reordered. To cope with such phenomena, so called promising semantics have been developed, which permit threads to read values that have not yet been written. Each promise is a speculative update that is later validated (fulfilled) by an actual write. Promising semantics are operational, providing a pathway for developing proof calculi. In this paper, we develop an incorrectness-style logic, resulting in a framework for reasoning about state reachability. Like incorrectness logic, our assertions are underapproximating , since the set of all valid promises are not known at the start of execution. Our logic uses event structures as assertions to compactly represent the ordering among events such as promised and fulfilled writes. We prove soundness and completeness of our proof calculus and demonstrate its applicability by proving reachability properties of standard weak memory litmus tests.

1
institutetext:
University of Oldenburg, Oldenburg, Germany

2
institutetext:
University of Surrey, Guildford, UK

1
Introduction

In recent years, numerous works have looked into semantics for
weak memory models for various hardware architectures or languages, e.g. for
x86-TSO
[
36
]
,
C11
[
2
,
27
]
,
Power
[
35
]
or
ARM
[
16
]
. Such semantics typically can
be classified as either being declarative (aka axiomatic) or
operational. Operational semantics furthermore can be divided into
those following a microarchitectural style (providing formalizations
of the actual hardware architecture) and those trying to abstract from
architectures. Most notably,
view-based

semantics
[
21
,
14
,
31
]

avoid modelling specific hardware components and instead define the
semantics in terms of
views
of thread on the shared state.
Promises

[
22
,
24
]
are employed
in operational semantics as a way of capturing out-of-order writes
while still executing operations in thread order. A promise (w.r.t. a
value
\kappa
and a shared location
x
) of a thread
\tau
states that
\tau

will eventually write value
\kappa
onto location
x
. All promised writes
then need to be
fulfilled
(i.e., justified) in the future of a program
run, but other threads can read from promises before they are
fulfilled.

Our
interest here is the development and use of
Hoare-style
[
18
]
structural proof calculi
(and their extensions to concurrency by Owicki and
Gries
[
29
]
) for weak memory models.
Owicki-Gries-like proof calculi have been proposed by a number of
researchers
[
23
,
11
,
12
,
41
]
,
and have also recently been given for non-volatile
memory
[
33
,
3
]
. Svendsen et
al.
[
37
]
have developed a separation
logic for promises for the C11 memory model. Wright et al.
[
41
]
have developed an Owicki-Gries proof system for out-of-order writes (as allowed by promises), but rely on pre-processing via the denotational MRD framework
[
30
]
.

All of these proposals follow Hoare’s principle of providing
safety

proofs. In particular, a Hoare triple

{\color[rgb]{0.0,0.5,0.0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.0,0.5,0.0}\big{\{}p\big{\}}}S{\color[rgb]{0.0,0.5,0.0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.0,0.5,0.0}\big{\{}q\big{\}}}
describes the fact that an execution of program
S

starting in a state satisfying
p
is either non-terminating, or
terminates in a state satisfying
q
(over-approximating the final
states). However, for weak memory models, we often want to prove

reachability
, i.e. under-approximate the set of final states, like in
the recent proposal of O’Hearn’s incorrectness
logic
[
28
]
. Here, a triple

{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}\big{[}p\big{]}}S{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}\big{[}q\big{]}}

describes the possibility of program
S
reaching all states
satisfying
q
when started in a state satisfying
p
. A verification
technique supporting these
reachability
triples enables one to
reason about executions that deviate from the expected sequentially consistent
behaviour of concurrent programs.

Contributions.

In this paper, we present a reachability proof calculus for concurrent
programs where the semantics of the weak memory model is based on
promises. The specific challenges therein lay in (i) capturing the
meaning of promises as writes which will only happen in the future but
can nevertheless already be read from, and (ii) appropriately
describing the required ordering (and concurrency) between
promises and fulfills as fixed by the concurrent program under
consideration. We address these challenges via the following contributions.

(1)

We develop a program logic based on assertions which are (flow)
event
structures

[
40
,
6
,
17
]
, employing
parallel composition of event structure and synchronization as a means
of determining whether all promises read from have eventually been
fulfilled.

(2)

We extend the theory of flow event structures with the notion of
a flow label to capture the behaviours observed in weak memory
models.

(3)

We develop the first
compositional
proof rule for a
concurrent reachability (incorrectness) logic.

(4)

We prove soundness and completeness of this novel event-structure based proof calculus.

(5)

Finally, we demonstrate its applicability on a number of litmus tests.

Overview.
In
Section

2
, we provide a
concrete overview via a motivating example and in

Section

3
, we present the memory model that we
use. Our model is a simplified (strengthened) version of the
ARMv8/RISC-V semantics of Pulte et al
[
31
]
. In

Section

4
, we present an extended theory for event
structures (specifically an extension of flow event structures) that has been designed to enable reasoning about relaxed memory models. We describe our reasoning methodology and provide examples verifying common litmus tests in
Section

5
.

2
Motivating Examples

Consider the program in
Fig.

2
, which describes
the load buffering litmus test. Thread 1 (similarly thread 2) loads
the value of
y
(sim.
x
) into register
a
(sim.
b
),
then updates
x
(sim.
y
) to
1
. Since there are no dependencies
between lines 1 and 2, and similarly between lines 3 and 4,
architectures such as ARMv8 and RISC-V allow the stores in both
threads to be reordered with the loads. Thus the program allows the
final outcome
a=1\wedge b=1
.

This phenomenon is captured by promising semantics by allowing each thread to “promise” their respective stores, then later fulfilling them. In the meantime, other threads may read from promised writes.
Our assertions within a thread reflect this semantics via assertions

{\cal E}
which are
flow event structures

[
40
]
. The events and their partial order reflect program executions, and in particular describe the various
views
which threads have on shared state.

\begin{array}[]{l@{}||@{}l}\begin{array}[t]{l}\textbf{Thread }1\\
{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}\big{[}\ \mathsf{ini}\ \big{]}}\\
1:a:=\textnormal{{load}}\,y;\\
{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}\big{[}\ \mathsf{ini}\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{rd}_{2}(y,1)\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{bar}(a,y)\ \big{]}}\\
2:\textnormal{{store}}\,\,x\,\,1;\\

{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}\big{[}\dots\big{]}}\\
4:b:=\textnormal{{load}}\,x;\\

{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}\big{[}\dots\big{]}}\end{array}\end{array}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\big{[}\ {\cal E}\ \big{]}}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\big{(}a=1\wedge b=1\big{)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\text{ \char 55}}}

Figure 2:
Load buffering with barriers

The proof outlines (i.e., program texts with assertions) of individual threads may first of all contain read events for arbitrary promises, i.e. describe the reading of arbitrary values.
In Thread 1 of
Fig.

2
, the pre-assertion of the load only
contains an event for initial writes (labelled
\mathsf{ini}
), yet the load may read the value
1
for

y
from a promised write, described by the event labelled
\mathsf{rd}_{2}(y,1)
in
the post assertion. The semantics
generates dependencies if the same register is used (perhaps
indirectly) by a read and a later write. This is captured in our
assertions using the event labelled
\mathsf{bar}(a,y)
, causally ordered after
\mathsf{rd}_{2}(y,1)
, which states that the
view of register
a
is at least that of the read of

y
.
Execution of line 2 then adds a fulfill event
with label
\mathsf{ff}_{1}(x,1)
to the assertion, which is not ordered with any other event except
\mathsf{ini}
. Symmetric
assertions can be generated for Thread 2. To obtain an
assertion describing the combined execution, we compose the final
event structures of both threads to obtain a “postcondition” of the
program. For this, we use parallel composition of event structures, synchronising read with their corresponding fulfill events. In
Fig.

2
, both reads are valid since the promises that these reads rely on
can be fulfilled in the composition without creating cyclic dependencies.

Fig.

2
presents a variation of the program in
Fig.

2
, which includes additional barriers
dmb
(fences) between the load and store in each thread, preventing their reordering. Again we build a proof outline for an execution in which Thread 1 loads
1
into
a
, obtaining the assertions shown. Note that here the event structure contains an additional fence event,
\mathsf{fnc}
, that is ordered after
\mathsf{bar}(a,y)
and before
\mathsf{ff}_{1}(x,1)
. Similarly, for Thread 2 loading
1
into
b
, we would obtain a symmetric set of assertions. Here, the parallel composition of local assertions is however not
interference free
(see below): the promises that threads 1 and 2 have read from cannot be fulfilled in this concurrent program. More detailedly, let
{\cal E}_{1}
and
{\cal E}_{2}
below be the (final) event structures of threads 1 and 2, respectively, where
\twoheadrightarrow
arrow denotes ordering and we now give event names together with labels.

{\cal E}_{1}
:

e_{\mathsf{ini}}:\mathsf{ini}\twoheadrightarrow e_{1}:\mathsf{rd}_{2}(y,1)\twoheadrightarrow e_{2}:\mathsf{bar}(a,y)\twoheadrightarrow e_{3}:\mathsf{fnc}_{1}\twoheadrightarrow e_{4}:\mathsf{ff}_{1}(x,1)

{\cal E}_{2}
:

f_{\mathsf{ini}}:\mathsf{ini}\twoheadrightarrow f_{1}:\mathsf{rd}_{1}(x,1)\twoheadrightarrow f_{2}:\mathsf{bar}(b,x)\twoheadrightarrow f_{3}:\mathsf{fnc}_{2}\twoheadrightarrow f_{4}:\mathsf{ff}_{2}(y,1)

To reason about the set of reachable final states of the concurrent program, we again construct the parallel composition of
{\cal E}_{1}
and
{\cal E}_{2}
(denoted
{\cal E}_{1}\|{\cal E}_{2}
):

(e_{\mathsf{ini}},f_{\mathsf{ini}}):\mathsf{ini}
(e_{1},f_{4}):\mathsf{ff}_{2}(y,1)
(e_{4},f_{1}):\mathsf{ff}_{1}(x,1)
(e_{1},*):\mathsf{rd}_{2}(y,1)
(e_{2},*):\mathsf{bar}(a,y)
(e_{3},*):\mathsf{fnc}_{1}
(e_{4},*):\mathsf{ff}_{1}(x,1)
(*,f_{1}):\mathsf{rd}_{1}(x,1)
(*,f_{2}):\mathsf{bar}(b,x)
(*,f_{3}):\mathsf{fnc}_{2}
(*,f_{4}):\mathsf{ff}_{2}(y,1)

This composition of event structure is built similar to

[
17
]
, allowing events of the parallel composition to be
lifted from the sub-components. These are events of the form

(e_{i},*)
and
(*,f_{i})
. The parallel composition also
contains
synchronised
read/fulfill events, e.g.,
(e_{1},f_{4})
depicts a
read synchronised with the fulfill (write)
\mathsf{ff}_{1}(y,1)
. We inherit
order in the composition from the constituent event
structures. Moreover, to prevent the same event occurring more than
once in an “execution” of
{\cal E}_{1}\|{\cal E}_{2}
, we use the
conflict

relation (zigzagged line). Thus, the synchronised event

(e_{1},f_{4})
conflicts with both
(e_{1},*)
and
(*,f_{4})
.

The final step in proving is the generation of a
valid interference free
configuration
of the parallel composition, which is a subset of the event structure
satisfying certain conditions, including acyclicity of
\twoheadrightarrow
,
absence of conflicts and absence of unsynchronised reads (ensuring the fulfillment of all promises read from). It turns out that for the event structure above, it is
impossible to generate such a configuration. The event
(e_{1},*)

cannot be included since it is an unsynchronised read. Therefore,

(e_{1},f_{4})
must be included. However, by the definition of a
configuration, this also means that the downclosure of
(e_{1},f_{4})

must be included, which results in a cycle:

(e_{1},f_{4})\twoheadrightarrow(e_{2},*)\twoheadrightarrow(e_{3},*)\twoheadrightarrow(e_{4},f_{1})\twoheadrightarrow(*,f_{2})\twoheadrightarrow(*,f_{3})\twoheadrightarrow(e_{1},f_{4})
. Since
{\cal E}_{1}\|{\cal E}_{2}
has no interference free configurations, the proof outline is not valid and in fact, a final state with

a=1\wedge b=1
is unreachable here.

3
A Weak Memory Semantics with Promises

We develop a promising semantics inspired by the recent view-based
operational semantics by Pulte et
al.
[
31
]
. We have reduced architecture-specific details,
allowing us to focus on the interaction between promises and thread
views. Our notion of a
promise
coincides with earlier works

[
31
,
22
,
24
]
. Threads can
promise to write certain values on shared locations and other threads
can read from this promise even before the actual write has
occurred. All promises however need to be fulfilled at the end of the
program execution.

Syntax.
Let
x,y\in\textsc{Loc}
be the set of
shared locations
,
\kappa\in\textsc{Val}
the set of
values
,
\tau\in\textsc{Tid}
the set of
thread identifiers
and
a,b\in\textsc{Reg}

local registers
. Our sequential language encompasses the following constructs:

\displaystyle{\it rv}

\displaystyle::=

\displaystyle\kappa\mid a\qquad\qquad st::=\textnormal{{skip}}\mid a:=\textnormal{{load}}\,x\mid\textnormal{{store}}\,\,x\,\,{\it rv}\mid a:=\eta\mid\textnormal{{dmb}}

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle::=

\displaystyle st\mid S;S\mid\textnormal{{asm}}\,\beta\mid S+S\mid S^{*}

where
\eta\in\textsc{Exp}
is an arithmetic and
\beta\in\textsc{BExp}
is a
boolean expressions, both over (local) registers only. We assume

S^{*}=\exists n\in\mathbb{N}.\ S^{n}
, where
S^{0}\mathrel{\widehat{=}}\textnormal{{skip}}
and

S^{n}\mathrel{\widehat{=}}S;S^{n-1}
. We use abbreviations:

\textnormal{{while}}\,\beta\,\textnormal{{do}}\,S=(\textnormal{{asm}}\,\beta;S)^{*};\textnormal{{asm}}\,\neg\beta
and

\textnormal{{if}}\,\beta\,\textnormal{{then}}\,S_{1}\,\textnormal{{else}}\,S_{2}=(\textnormal{{asm}}\,\beta;S_{1})+(\textnormal{{asm}}\,\neg\beta;S_{2})
, where

\textnormal{{asm}}\,\beta\,
is a command that tests whether
\beta
holds.

Timestamped state.
We let
{\it TState}
be the set of all
timestamped states
and
\mathit{Memory}
the set of all memory states, both of which we make more precise below.
A
thread

T\in{\it Thread}
is an element of
S\times{\it TState}
, a
concurrent program
is a mapping
\vec{T}\in{\it TPool}\ \mathrel{\widehat{=}}\ \textsc{Tid}\rightarrow{\it Thread}
and a
concurrent program state
is a pair
\langle\vec{T},M\rangle\in\mathit{TPool}\times\mathit{Memory}
.
We let
R(\tau)
,
\tau\in\textsc{Tid}
, be the set of registers occurring in the program of
\vec{T}(\tau)
. We assume
R(\tau)\cap R(\tau^{\prime})=\emptyset
whenever
\tau\neq\tau^{\prime}
.

Threads will make
promises
for writes at particular timestamps. Timestamps
t\in\mathbb{T}
are natural numbers.
We define
t\sqcup t^{\prime}\ \mathrel{\widehat{=}}\ max(t,t^{\prime})
and generalise this to sets of timestamps using
\bigsqcup_{t\in T}t
, where
\bigsqcup_{t\in\emptyset}t=0
.
A
memory
is a sequence of write messages of type
{\sf Wr}\ \mathrel{\widehat{=}}\ (\textsc{Loc}\times\textsc{Val}\times\textsc{Tid})\cup\{\mathsf{ini}\}
, where
\mathsf{ini}
is a special write message denoting initialisation. The position of a write in the sequence fixes its timestamp.
We assume all variables are initialised with value
0
.

We denote a write
w\ \mathrel{\widehat{=}}\ (x,\kappa,\tau)
using
\langle x:=\kappa\rangle_{\tau}
and let
w.loc=x,w.val=\kappa
and
w.tid=\tau
. For a memory
M
and thread
\tau\in\textsc{Tid}
, we let
M_{\tau}\subseteq{\mathbb{T}}

be the set of timestamps of entries of
\tau
in
M
, i.e.
\{t\in\mathbb{T}\mid M(t).tid=\tau\}
.
M_{\tau}
is used to determine the promise set of each
\tau
. We write
tids(M)
to denote the set of threads with entries in
M
. New messages
w
are appended at the end of the memory, which we write as
M\mathbin{+\!\!\!+}w
.

A thread state
ts\in{\it TState}
consists of the following components: a set of (non-fulfilled)
promises

prom\in 2^{\mathbb{T}}
, a
coherence
view of each location,
coh:\textsc{Loc}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
, the value and view of each register,
{\it regs}:\textsc{Reg}\rightarrow\textsc{Val}\times\mathbb{T}
, a read view
v_{\mathit{read}}:\mathbb{T}
, two write views
v_{\mathit{wOld}},v_{\mathit{wNew}}:\mathbb{T}
and a condition view
v_{C}:\mathbb{T}
. We write
{\it regs}(a)
as
\kappa@v
and also let
v_{a}
be this view
v
of register
a
.
Finally, the evaluation of an expression
\eta
with respect to a register assignment
{\it regs}
,
\llbracket\eta\rrbracket_{\it regs}\in\textsc{Val}\times\mathbb{T}
, is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket\kappa\rrbracket_{\it regs}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\widehat{=}}\kappa@0\text{ for }\kappa\in\textsc{Val},\qquad\llbracket a\rrbracket_{\it regs}\mathrel{\widehat{=}}{\it regs}(a)\text{ for }a\in\textsc{Reg},

\displaystyle\llbracket\eta_{1}\mathop{op}\eta_{2}\rrbracket_{\it regs}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\widehat{=}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l}(\kappa_{1}\llbracket op\rrbracket\kappa_{2})@(v_{1}\sqcup v_{2})\text{ with }\llbracket\eta_{1}\rrbracket_{\it regs}=\kappa_{1}@v_{1},\llbracket\eta_{2}\rrbracket_{\it regs}=\kappa_{2}@v_{2}\end{array}

Note that this evaluation is with respect to the register function
{\it regs}
and this calculates both the value of the expression and the maximal view of the registers within the expression.

To define the initial state of a program, we let

\displaystyle M_{\mathsf{ini}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\widehat{=}}\langle\mathsf{ini}\rangle

\displaystyle ts_{\mathsf{ini}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\widehat{=}}\left[\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}prom=\{\},v_{read}=v_{wNew}=v_{wOld}=v_{C}=0,\\
coh=(\lambda x.\ 0),{\it regs}=(\lambda a.\ 0@0)\end{array}\right]

where
ts_{\mathsf{ini}}
is a record initialising the promises to the empty set, each view to
0
, the coherence function to a map from locations to timestamp
0
, and the register function to a map from registers to value
0
with timestamp
0
.
We say that a program
\vec{T}
is locally in its initial state iff for each thread
\tau
, we have
\pi_{2}(\vec{T}(\tau))=ts_{\mathsf{ini}}
, where
\pi_{i}
projects the
i
th component of a tuple. Given that
\vec{T}
is in its initial state, the initial concurrent program state is given by

\langle\vec{T},M_{\mathsf{ini}}\rangle
.

\inferrule[Promise]{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}w.tid=\tau\quad w.loc=x\quad w.val=\kappa\\
t=|M|+1\\
ts^{\prime}=ts[prom\mapsto ts.prom\cup\{t\}]\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{l}\big{\langle}\langle S,ts\rangle,M\big{\rangle}\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle prm(x,\kappa)}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}_{\tau}\big{\langle}\langle S,ts^{\prime}\rangle,M\mathbin{+\!\!\!+}w\big{\rangle}\end{array}}\quad\inferrule[Fence]{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}v=ts.v_{read}\sqcup ts.v_{wOld}\\
ts^{\prime}=ts\left[\begin{array}[c]{l}v_{read}\mapsto v\\
v_{\mathit{wNew}}\mapsto v\end{array}\right]\end{array}}{\big{\langle}\langle\textnormal{{dmb}},ts\rangle,M\big{\rangle}\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{fnc}}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}_{\tau}\big{\langle}\langle\textnormal{{skip}},ts^{\prime}\rangle,M\big{\rangle}}

\inferrule[Read]{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}M(t).loc=x\qquad M(t).val=\kappa\\
\forall t^{\prime}.\ t<t^{\prime}\leq(ts.v_{read}\sqcup ts.coh(x))\Rightarrow\\
\qquad M(t^{\prime}).loc\neq x\\
v_{post}=ts.v_{read}\sqcup t\\
ts^{\prime}=ts\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}regs(a)\mapsto\kappa@v_{post},\\
coh(x)\mapsto ts.coh(x)\sqcup v_{post},\\
v_{read}\mapsto v_{post}\end{array}\right]\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\big{\langle}\langle a:=\textnormal{{load}}\,x,ts\rangle,M\big{\rangle}\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle rd(x,\kappa)}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}_{\tau}\\
\hfill\big{\langle}\langle\textnormal{{skip}},ts^{\prime}\rangle,M\big{\rangle}\end{array}}\quad\inferrule[Fulfill]{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}t\in ts.prom\quad\llbracket{\it rv}\rrbracket_{ts.regs}=\kappa@v_{\it rv}\\
M(t)=\langle x:=\kappa\rangle_{\tau}\\
ts.v_{\mathit{wNew}}\sqcup ts.v_{C}\sqcup ts.coh(x)\sqcup v_{\it rv}<t\\
ts^{\prime}=ts\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}prom\mapsto ts.prom\setminus\{t\},\\
coh(x)\mapsto t,\\
v_{\mathit{wOld}}\mapsto v_{\mathit{wOld}}\sqcup t\end{array}\right]\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\big{\langle}\langle\textnormal{{store}}\,\,x\,\,{\it rv},ts\rangle,M\big{\rangle}\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\it ff}(x,\kappa)}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}_{\tau}\\
\hfill\big{\langle}\langle\textnormal{{skip}},ts^{\prime}\rangle,M\big{\rangle}\end{array}}

\hfill\inferrule[Register]{regs(a)=\kappa_{a}@u\qquad\llbracket\eta\rrbracket_{ts.regs}=\kappa@v\qquad ts^{\prime}=ts\left[\begin{array}[c]{l}regs(a)\mapsto\kappa@(u\sqcup v)\\
\end{array}\right]}{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\big{\langle}\langle a:=\eta,ts\rangle,M\big{\rangle}\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathit{lst}(a,\eta)}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}_{\tau}\big{\langle}\langle\textnormal{{skip}},ts^{\prime}\rangle,M\big{\rangle}\end{array}}\hfill

\inferrule[Assume]{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\llbracket\beta\rrbracket_{ts.regs}=\mathsf{true}@v\\
ts^{\prime}=ts[v_{C}\mapsto ts.v_{C}\sqcup v]\end{array}}{\big{\langle}\langle\textnormal{{asm}}\,\beta,ts\rangle,M\big{\rangle}\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle asm(\beta)}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}_{\tau}\big{\langle}\langle\textnormal{{skip}},ts^{\prime}\rangle,M\big{\rangle}}\quad\inferrule[ProgramStep]{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\langle\vec{T}(\tau),M\rangle\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle op}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}_{\tau}\langle T^{\prime},M\rangle\\
\langle T^{\prime},M\rangle\text{ certifiable }\end{array}}{\langle\vec{T},M\rangle\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle op}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}_{\tau}\langle\vec{T}[\tau\mapsto T^{\prime}],M^{\prime}\rangle}

Figure 3:
Operational semantics (Atomic statement rules)

The rules of the operational semantics (except for standard rules for program constructs) are given in
Fig.

3
. The two key rules are the
Read
and
Fulfill
rule.
Read
identifies a timestamp
t
to read a value for
x
from such that in between
t
and the maximum of read view and coherence of
x
, there are no further promises to
x
in memory
M
. It updates read view, coherence of
x
and the view of the register involved in the load as to ensure preservation of dependencies.
Fulfill
fulfills an already made promise (to write
\kappa
to
x
) of a thread at timestamp
t
, and to this end has to ensure that views
v_{\mathit{wNew}},v_{C},coh(x)
as well as that of the value/register are less than
t
. It removes
t
from the thread’s promise set and updates
coh(x)
and
v_{\mathit{wOld}}
(as to ensure dependencies with fences).
Rule
Promise
simply adds an arbitrary new promise at the end of memory.
Fence
ensures views
v_{\mathit{read}}
and
v_{\mathit{wNew}}
are updated. This rule for instance guarantees that store operations separated by barriers
dmb
can only be fulfilled in that order, i.e. the write of the first store cannot be promised to happen later than the write of the second store (more precisely, such promises cannot be fulfilled).

Finally, we say that
\langle T,M\rangle
is
certifiable
(used in
Program Step
) if there is
some
T^{\prime},M^{\prime}
such that

\langle T,M\rangle\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}^{*}_{\tau}\langle T^{\prime},M^{\prime}\rangle
and

T^{\prime}.prom=\emptyset
. Certifiability ensures that a concurrent program can only make steps when all promises can eventually be fulfilled. Like
[
31
]
, in our semantics, all promise steps can be done at the beginning without losing any of the reachable states (see
Lemma

1
).

4
Event Structures

Event structures
[
40
,
5
,
6
,
17
]
are
models of concurrent systems which compactly represent (concurrent)
executions. Here, we use
flow event structures because of their ease in defining a
compositional parallel composition
[
17
]
.

Notation.

Event structures consist of sets of
events

d,e,f\in E
.
Events will be labelled with
actions
which are here specific to our usage and give us information about program executions:

\displaystyle\mathit{Act}^{x}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\widehat{=}}\bigcup_{\tau\in\textsc{Tid},\kappa\in\textsc{Val}}\{\mathsf{rd}_{\tau}(x,\kappa),\mathsf{ff}_{\tau}(x,\kappa)\}\cup\{\mathsf{ini}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{Act}^{\mathsf{fnc}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\widehat{=}}\bigcup_{\tau\in\textsc{Tid}}\{\mathsf{fnc}_{\tau}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{Act}^{a}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\widehat{=}}\bigcup_{x\in\textsc{Loc},\eta\in\textsc{Exp}}\{\mathsf{bar}(a,x),\mathsf{bar}(a,\eta)\}

\displaystyle\mathit{Act}^{\mathsf{tst}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\widehat{=}}\bigcup_{\tau\in\textsc{Tid},\beta\in\textsc{BExp}}\{\mathsf{tst}_{\tau}(\beta)\}

Actions on a location
x
can be
read
actions
\mathsf{rd}_{\tau}(\cdot,\cdot)
,
fulfill
actions
\mathsf{ff}_{\tau}(\cdot,\cdot)
or the initialization
\mathsf{ini}
. Note that the thread identifier
\tau
in read actions is the id of the thread having made the promise and in fulfill actions it is the thread executing the fulfill (and having made the corresponding promise). We let
\mathit{Act}^{\mathsf{rd}}
denote all read and
\mathit{Act}^{\mathsf{ff}}
all fulfill actions. To record loading into register
a
, we use so called
bar
actions
\mathsf{bar}(a,\cdot)
. The action
\mathsf{fnc}
occurs when a
dmb
statement is executed
and
\mathsf{tst}_{\cdot}(\cdot)
describes the execution of some
asm
statement.

We often lift notations to sets of locations
L\subseteq\textsc{Loc}
or sets of registers
R\subseteq\textsc{Reg}
. For example,
\mathit{Act}^{L}=\bigcup_{x\in L}\mathit{Act}^{x}
. The overall set of actions is
\mathit{Act}=\mathit{Act}^{\textsc{Loc}}\cup\mathit{Act}^{\textsc{Reg}}\cup\mathit{Act}^{\mathsf{fnc}}\cup\mathit{Act}^{\mathsf{tst}}
.

Definition 1

A
location-coloured flow event structure
(short: event structure)
{\cal E}=(E,\twoheadrightarrow,\#,\Lambda,\ell)
labelled over a set of actions
Act
consists of

a finite set of events
E
,

an irreflexive
flow relation

\mathord{\twoheadrightarrow}\subseteq E\times E
,

a
location restriction
function
\Lambda:E\times E\to 2^{{\textsc{Loc}}}
,

a symmetric
conflict relation

\#\subseteq E\times E
, and

a
labelling function

\ell:E\rightarrow Act
.

For
L\subseteq\textsc{Loc}
, we write
e\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}}}f
to denote

e\twoheadrightarrow f
and
\Lambda(e,f)=L
. The location restrictions are employed to reflect the application condition of rule
Read
within the event structure: it tells us that there is no write to
x\in L
in between
e
and
f
, where
e
and
f
will eventually be mapped to timestamps in memory.

We let
\mathsf{Ini}
be the event
structure
(\{e_{\mathsf{ini}}\},\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\ell)
with

\ell(e_{\mathsf{ini}})=\mathsf{ini}
.
Given an event structure

{\cal E}=(E,\twoheadrightarrow,\#,\Lambda,\ell)
, we – similarly to actions
– define its set of events labelled with specific actions as

\mathsf{Rd}({\cal E})
,
\mathsf{Rd}_{\tau}({\cal E})
,
\mathsf{Rd}_{\tau}^{x}({\cal E})
,
\mathsf{Ff}({\cal E})
,

\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}({\cal E})
and
\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}^{x}({\cal E})

via the labelling function
\ell
. For an event
e
labelled with
an action in
\mathit{Act}^{x}\backslash\{\mathsf{ini}\}
, we let
e.loc=x
. We slightly abuse notation so that
e_{\mathsf{ini}}.loc=x
for all
x
. We furthermore define

last_{\alpha}({\cal E})
,
\alpha\in Act
, to be the last event in flow order
labelled
\alpha
, i.e.,
last_{\alpha}({\cal E})=e
if
\ell(e)=\alpha
and
for all
e^{\prime}
such that
e^{\prime}\neq e
and
\ell(e^{\prime})=\alpha
, we have
e^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{+}e
. Moreover,

last_{\alpha}({\cal E})=\bot
if no event labelled
\alpha
exists.
We lift

last
to sets of actions by

last_{A}({\cal E})=\{last_{\alpha}({\cal E})\mid\alpha\in\mathit{Act}\}
.
An event structure
{\cal E}
is
sequential
if all events are flow-ordered:
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in E,e\neq e^{\prime}:e\twoheadrightarrow^{+}e^{\prime}\vee e^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{+}e
. We let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of sequential event structures.

An event structure describes (several) concurrent executions in compact form. One execution is therein given as a configuration.

Definition 2

A
configuration
C\subseteq E
of an event structure
{\cal E}=(E,\twoheadrightarrow,\#,\Lambda,\ell)
satisfies the following properties:

(1)
C
is cycle-free:
(\twoheadrightarrow\cap(C\times C))^{+}
is irreflexive,

(2)
C
is conflict-free:
\#\cap(C\times C)=\emptyset
,

(3)
C
is left-closed up to conflicts:
\forall d,e\in E
, if
e\in C
,
d\twoheadrightarrow e
and
d\notin C
, then there exists
f\in C
such that
d\#f
and
f\twoheadrightarrow e
.

We let
\mathit{Conf}({\cal E})
be the set of configurations of
{\cal E}
. We identify a configuration with the (conflict-free) event structure
{\cal E}_{C}
which is
{\cal E}
restricted to events of
C
.

Our intention is to use event structures to record information about the local history of each thread, in particular the promises of other threads which they have read from. Eventually (i.e., when combining local event structures) all promises read from need to be fulfilled. This is captured by our notion of parallel composition which requires fulfills (of a thread
\tau
) to
synchronize
with reads from promises of
\tau
. Similary to CCS
[
26
]
, we model this synchronisation via
complementary
actions where
\overline{\mathsf{rd}_{\tau}(x,\kappa)}=\mathsf{ff}_{\tau}(x,\kappa)
and vice versa, and
\overline{\overline{a}}=a
. Contrary to CCS, the synchronisation does not create internal actions, but keeps the fulfill labels (as to still see what promise a fulfill belonged to).

We first define the
synchronising events
of
n
event structures
{\cal E}_{1}
, …,
{\cal E}_{n}
, as follows, where
E_{i*}
denotes
E_{i}\cup\{*\}
.

sync({\cal E}_{1},\dots,{\cal E}_{n})\mathrel{\widehat{=}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\left\{\begin{array}[c]{@{}l}(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n})\in E_{1*}\times E_{2*}\times\dots\times E_{n*}\mid{}\\
\exists i.\ \ell_{i}(e_{i})\in\mathit{Act}^{\mathsf{ff}}\wedge(\forall j\neq i.\ e_{j}\neq*\Rightarrow\overline{\ell_{i}(e_{i})}=\ell_{j}(e_{j}))\wedge{}\\
\qquad(\exists j\neq i.\ e_{j}\neq*)\end{array}\right\}\\
{}\cup\{(e_{\mathsf{ini}}^{1},e_{\mathsf{ini}}^{2},\dots,e_{\mathsf{ini}}^{n})\}\end{array}

An event
e
might also occur unsynchronized in a parallel composition (which is then written as
(*,\dots,*,e,*,\dots,*)
.

Note that since we aim to reason about reachability of states
(underapproximation), we just need parallel composition for
conflict-free
event structures, i.e. for event structures
describing a single execution. Thus the
\Delta
-axiom of Castellani
and Zhang
[
7
]
which they impose in order to get
compositionality is trivially fulfilled for our application. Next, we
still first of all define parallel composition of arbitrary event
structures.

We let
\times_{i}S
denote the product
S\times S\times\dots S
generating a tuple of length
i
. If
i\leq 0
, we let
\times_{i}S=\bot
. Finally, we let
\bot\times S=S\times\bot=S
.

Definition 3
(Parallel composition)

Let
{\cal E}_{1},{\cal E}_{2},\dots,{\cal E}_{n}
be event structures for threads
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
, …,
\tau_{n}
, respectively.
The
parallel composition

{\cal E}={\cal E}_{1}||{\cal E}_{2}||\dots||{\cal E}_{n}
is the event structure
(E,\twoheadrightarrow,\#,\Lambda,\ell)
with

•

\begin{array}[t]{@{}rcl@{}}E&=&sync({\cal E}_{1},{\cal E}_{2},\dots\cal E_{n})\cup\big{(}\bigcup_{i}(\times_{i-1}\{*\})\times(E_{i}\setminus\{e^{i}_{\mathsf{ini}}\})\times(\times_{n-i}\{*\})\big{)}\end{array}

•

(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n})\twoheadrightarrow(d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{n})
iff
\exists i.\ e_{i}\twoheadrightarrow_{i}d_{i}
,

•

\Lambda((e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n}),(d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{n}))=\bigcup_{i}\Lambda(e_{i},d_{i})
,

•

(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n})\#(d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{n})
iff

–

\exists i.\ e_{i}\#_{i}d_{i}
, or (inherit conflicts)

–

\exists i,j.\ e_{i}=d_{i}\wedge e_{i}\neq*\wedge e_{j}\neq d_{j}
(conflicts on differently paired events),

•

Labels:

\ell(e_{1},e_{2},\dots e_{n})=\begin{cases}\mathsf{ini}&\text{if $(e_{1},e_{2},\dots e_{n})=(e_{\mathsf{ini}}^{1},e_{\mathsf{ini}}^{2},\dots e_{\mathsf{ini}}^{n})$}\\
\ell(e_{i})&\text{if $(e_{1},e_{2},\dots e_{n})\in sync({\cal E}_{1},{\cal E}_{2},\dots\cal E_{n})\wedge\ell(e_{i})=\mathsf{ff}_{\cdot}(\cdot,\cdot)$}\\
\ell(e_{i})&\text{if $(e_{1},e_{2},\dots e_{n})\notin sync({\cal E}_{1},{\cal E}_{2},\dots\cal E_{n})\wedge e_{i}\neq*$}\end{cases}

Parallel composition of event structures is used to combine local
proof outlines of threads. This combination is only possible if
enough synchronization partners are available. Event structures

{\cal E}_{1}
to
{\cal E}_{n}
are
synchronizable
if

\pi_{i}(sync({\cal E}_{1},\ldots,{\cal E}_{n}))

\supseteq\mathsf{Rd}(E_{i})
,

i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
(all the reads have a synchronization
with a fulfill). The configuration (describing an execution of the
parallel composition of threads) which we extract from

\mathit{Conf}({\cal E}_{1}||\ldots||{\cal E}_{n})
furthermore has to guarantee that
no events from the local proof outlines are lost and that the local
assertions make no contradictory assumptions about the contents
of memory.

Definition 4

The event structure
{\cal E}_{C}=(E_{C},\twoheadrightarrow_{C},\emptyset,\Lambda_{C},\ell_{C})
corresponding to a configuration
C\in\mathit{Conf}({\cal E}_{1}||\ldots||{\cal E}_{n})
is
interference free
if

1.

C
is
thread-covering
:
\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}:\pi_{i}(E_{C})=E_{i}
,

2.

C
is
memory-consistent linearizable
: there exists a total order
\mathord{\prec}\subseteq\mathit{Act}^{x}(E_{C})\times\mathit{Act}^{x}(E_{C})
among reads, fulfills and the
\mathsf{ini}
event such that

•

\mathord{\twoheadrightarrow_{C}}^{+}\cap(\mathit{Act}^{x}(E_{C})\times\mathit{Act}^{x}(E_{C}))\subseteq\mathord{\prec}
and

•

\forall d,e,f\in E_{C}
:
d\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}}}f\wedge d\prec e\prec f\implies e.loc\notin L
,

3.

C
contains
no unsynchronised reads
: there is no event in
E_{C}
of the form
(*,*,\dots,*,e_{i},*\dots,*)
, where
e_{i}\in\mathsf{Rd}({\cal E}_{i})
.

Example 1

Consider the two event structures given next (which belong to a message passing program with barriers, see
Section

5
).

e_{\mathsf{ini}}:\mathsf{ini}
e_{1}:\mathsf{ff}_{1}(x,5)
e_{2}:\mathsf{fnc}_{1}
e_{3}:\mathsf{ff}_{1}(y,1)

f_{\mathsf{ini}}:\mathsf{ini}
f_{1}:\mathsf{rd}_{1}(f,1)
f_{2}:\mathsf{bar}(a,y)
f_{3}:\mathsf{bar}(b,x)
{x}

Their parallel composition gives the following event structure:

(e_{\mathsf{ini}},f_{\mathsf{ini}}):\mathsf{ini}
(e_{1},*):\mathsf{ff}_{1}(x,5)
(e_{2},*):\mathsf{fnc}_{1}
(e_{3},*):\mathsf{ff}_{1}(y,1)
(e_{3},f_{1}):\mathsf{ff}_{1}(y,1)
(*,f_{1}):\mathsf{rd}_{1}(y,1)
(*,f_{2}):\mathsf{bar}(a,y)
(*,f_{3}):\mathsf{bar}(b,x)
\{x\}
\{x\}

This event structure has no interference-free configuration. To satisfy the conditions “thread-covering” and “no unsynchronised reads”, we must include event
(e_{3},f_{1})
. This means the only possible configuration must also include the downclosure
(e_{1},*)
and
(e_{2},*)
. However, together with the location restriction

\{x\}
on the edge
((e_{\mathsf{ini}},f_{\mathsf{ini}}),(e_{3},f_{1}))
, the resulting event structure is not memory-consistent linearizable, since it contains a sequence
(e_{\mathsf{ini}},f_{\mathsf{ini}})\twoheadrightarrow(e_{1},*)\twoheadrightarrow(e_{2},*)\twoheadrightarrow(e_{3},f_{1})
, where
(e_{1},*)
corresponds to a fulfilled write on
x
that is forbidden by the edge
((e_{\mathsf{ini}},f_{\mathsf{ini}})\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\{x\}}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}}}(e_{3},f_{1}))
. Conceptually, this means that we cannot find a memory
M
which matches the constraints on its contents given in the event structure.

5
Reasoning

Our overall objective is the design of a proof calculus for reasoning about the
reachability
of certain final states of concurrent programs.
A concurrent program state describes the values of registers and shared variables, the contents of memory and the views of threads.
During reasoning, we employ event structures as
assertions
in proof outlines.
They abstract from the concrete state in neither giving the exact contents of memory nor the timestamps of thread views.

5.1
Semantics of Assertions

Local assertions in the proof outlines of single threads take the form
{\cal E}
, where
{\cal E}
is a conflict-free event structure (i.e.,
\#=\emptyset
). The event structure is conflict-free because it describes a
single
execution of the thread (reachability logic).
An assertion for a thread
\tau
can have fence and fulfill events of
\tau
, read events reading from (promises of) threads
\tau^{\prime}\neq\tau
as well as bar and test events over registers of
R(\tau)
.
The events in
{\cal E}
– together with some memory
M
– allow us to compute the current views of threads.
Figure
4
gives some definitions for calculating views.

A local assertion of a thread
\tau
defines
constraints on the global memory
M
(the ordering of writes and
their values) as well as the views of
\tau
: An assertion
{\cal E}

describes a set of states

\llbracket{\cal E}\rrbracket=\{\langle\vec{ts},M\rangle\in(\textsc{Tid}\rightarrow{\it TState})\times{\it Memory}\mid\langle\vec{ts},M\rangle\text{ matches }{\cal E}\}
where “matches” is defined by conditions
(1)
-
(4)
below.

\displaystyle\mathit{prFnc}_{\tau}({\cal E})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{e\in\mathsf{Rd}({\cal E})\cup\mathsf{Ff}({\cal E})\cup\{e_{\mathsf{ini}}\}\mid\exists e^{\prime}\in last_{\mathsf{fnc}_{\tau}}({\cal E}):e\twoheadrightarrow^{+}e^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{prBar}_{a}({\cal E})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{e\in\mathsf{Rd}({\cal E})\cup\mathsf{Ff}({\cal E})\cup\{e_{\mathsf{ini}}\}\mid\exists e^{\prime}\in last_{\mathsf{bar}(a,\cdot)}({\cal E}):e\twoheadrightarrow^{+}e^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{prBar}_{\tau}({\cal E})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{a\in R(\tau)}\mathit{prBar}_{a}({\cal E})

\displaystyle\mathit{prBar}^{x}_{\tau}({\cal E})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathit{prBar}_{\tau}({\cal E})\cap\mathit{Act}^{x}({\cal E})

\displaystyle\mathit{prTst}_{\tau}({\cal E})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{e\in\mathsf{Rd}({\cal E})\cup\mathsf{Ff}({\cal E})\cup\{e_{\mathsf{ini}}\}\mid\exists e^{\prime}\in last_{\mathsf{tst}_{\tau}(\cdot)}({\cal E}):e\twoheadrightarrow^{+}e^{\prime}\}

Figure 4:
Determining the decisive reads and writes prior to an event (
{\cal E}=(E,\twoheadrightarrow,\#,\Lambda,\ell)
event structure,
\tau\in\textsc{Tid}
,
a\in\textsc{Reg},x\in\textsc{Loc}
)

(1)

M
is consistent with the fulfill and read events of
{\cal E}
.

There exists a total mapping
\psi:\mathsf{Ff}({\cal E})\cup\mathsf{Rd}({\cal E})\cup\{e_{\mathsf{ini}}\}\rightarrow dom(M)
which

1.

initializes at zero
: the one event
e_{\mathsf{ini}}
labelled
\mathsf{ini}
is mapped to 0,

2.

is
consecutive
for every thread
\tau
:

for all
e\in\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}({\cal E})
,
t\in\mathbb{T}
s.t.
M(t)=\langle x:=\kappa\rangle_{\tau}
,
t<\psi(e)
and
e.loc=x
, there exists
d\in\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}({\cal E})
such that
\psi(d)=t
,

3.

preserves content
: if
\psi(e)=t\neq 0
and
M(t)=\langle x:=\kappa\rangle_{\tau}
, then
\ell(e)\in\{\mathsf{ff}_{\tau}(x,\kappa),\mathsf{rd}_{\tau}(x,\kappa)\}
,

4.

preserves flows
:
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in dom(M):e\twoheadrightarrow^{+}_{\cal E}e^{\prime}\Rightarrow\psi(e)<\psi(e^{\prime})
,

5.

and
preserves memory constraints
:

\forall d,e\in dom(M)
,
L\subseteq\textsc{Loc}
s.t.
d\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}}}e
,
\forall t\in\mathbb{T}
s.t.
\psi(d)<t<\psi(e)
:
M(t).loc\neq d.loc
.

The mapping
\psi
is used to assign timestamps to read and fulfill events. We therefore will later also talk about the
timestamp of an event
(depending on such a mapping).
Note that the event structure
\mathsf{Ini}
is consistent with all memories
M
(using mapping
\psi(e_{\mathsf{ini}})=0
).

(2)
The open (non-fulfilled) promises of a thread
\tau
are
the entries of
\tau
in
M
which are not fulfilled, i.e.,

\vec{ts}(\tau).prom=M_{\tau}\setminus\psi(\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}({\cal E}))
.

(3)
The views of a thread
\tau
are consistent with mapping
\psi
and
M
.

Letting
ts=\vec{ts}(\tau)
,
a\in R(\tau)
and
x\in\textsc{Loc}
, we have

\displaystyle ts.v_{C}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{e\in\mathit{prTst}_{\tau}({\cal E})}\psi(e)

\displaystyle ts.coh(x)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{e\in\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}^{x}({\cal E})\cup\mathit{prBar}_{\tau}^{x}({\cal E})}\psi(e)

\displaystyle ts.v_{\mathit{wOld}}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{e\in\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}({\cal E})}\psi(e)

\displaystyle ts.v_{\mathit{wNew}}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{e\in\mathit{prFnc}_{\tau}({\cal E})\cap\big{(}\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}({\cal E})\cup\mathit{prBar}_{\tau}({\cal E})\big{)}}\psi(e)

\displaystyle zts.v_{a}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{e\in\mathit{prBar}_{a}({\cal E})}\psi(e)

\displaystyle ts.v_{read}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{e\in\big{(}\mathit{prFnc}_{\tau}({\cal E})\cap\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}({\cal E})\big{)}\cup\mathit{prBar}_{\tau}({\cal E})}\psi(e)

(4)
The values of registers
R(\tau)
of thread
\tau
agree with values in
{\cal E}
.

For
a\in\textsc{Reg}
,
ts.regs(a)=\kappa@v_{a}
with
\kappa=\llbracket a\rrbracket_{\cal E}
(where the semantics of a register
a
in
{\cal E}
is (1) 0 if no bar event for
a
is in
{\cal E}
or (2) the value of a read or fulfill to
x
prior to the last
\mathsf{bar}(a,\cdot)
(on
x
) or (3) the value of the expression
\eta
in a last
\mathsf{bar}(a,\eta)
) and
v_{a}
as defined above.

\ldots
0
6
9

\langle x,0\rangle

\langle y,0\rangle

\ldots
\langle y,1\rangle_{1}
\ldots
\langle z,3\rangle_{1}

no write to
x

in
M(1)
to
M(5)

\mathsf{ini}
\mathsf{ff}_{1}(y,1)
\mathsf{bar}(a,y)
\mathsf{bar}(b,x)
{x}

\begin{array}[t]{l}\textbf{Thread }1\\
1:\textnormal{{store}}\,\,y\,\,1;\\
2:a:=\textnormal{{load}}\,y;\\
3:b:=\textnormal{{load}}\,x;\\
4:\textnormal{{store}}\,\,z\,\,3;\end{array}

Figure 5:
Example memory
M
(left) for event structure
{\cal E}
(middle) describing an execution of statements 1, 2 and 3 in thread 1 (right).

State
ts
of thread 1:
prom=\{9\},v_{C}=v_{\mathit{wNew}}=coh(z)=0,v_{a}=v_{b}=coh(y)=coh(x)=v_{\mathit{wOld}}=v_{read}=6
, using mapping
\psi:\mathsf{ini}\mapsto 0,\mathsf{ff}_{1}(y,1)\mapsto 6
.

Figure
5
gives an example for the definition of “matches”. On the right hand side we see the program of
thread 1. It first stores 1 to
y
, then loads the values of
y
and
x
into registers
a
and
b
, respectively, and finally stores 3 to
z
. The event structure in the middle gives the assertion reached after statement 3, i.e.
before
the final store operation. The memory
M
on the left hand side matches this event structure: There are promises for the event
\mathsf{ini}
at
M(0)
as well as for event
\mathsf{ff}_{1}(y,1)
, so
\psi
maps
\mathsf{ini}
to 0 and
\mathsf{ff}_{1}(y,1)
to 6. The colored location restriction in the event structure furthermore requires not to have any promises to
x
in between 0 and 6. As there is one more promise of thread 1 in
M
, not yet covered by the event structure, we can derive
1.prom=\{9\}
.

5.2
Proof Rules

Essentially, assertions describe the events which have already happened together with their orderings plus further constraints.

\displaystyle{\cal E}\oplus\mathsf{ff}_{\tau}(x,\kappa)

\displaystyle=(E_{e},\mathord{\twoheadrightarrow}\cup\{(e^{\prime},e)\mid e^{\prime}\in last_{\mathit{Act}^{x}\cup\{\mathsf{fnc}_{\tau},\mathsf{tst}_{\tau}(\cdot)\}}({\cal E})\},\Lambda,\ell[e\mapsto\mathsf{ff}_{\tau}(x,\kappa))])

\displaystyle{\cal E}\oplus^{a}\mathsf{ff}_{\tau}(x,\kappa)

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}(E_{e},\mathord{\twoheadrightarrow}\cup\{(e^{\prime},e)\mid e^{\prime}\in last_{\mathit{Act}^{x}\cup\{\mathsf{fnc}_{\tau},\mathsf{tst}_{\tau}(\cdot),\mathsf{bar}(a,\cdot)\}}({\cal E})\},\\
\hfill\Lambda,\ell[e\mapsto\mathsf{ff}_{\tau}(x,\kappa)])\end{array}

\displaystyle{\cal E}\oplus\mathsf{bar}(a,x)

\displaystyle=(E_{e},\mathord{\twoheadrightarrow}\cup\{(e^{\prime},e)\mid e^{\prime}\in last_{\mathit{Act}^{x}\cup\{\mathsf{fnc}_{\tau},\mathsf{bar}(\cdot,\cdot)\}}({\cal E})\},\Lambda,\ell[e\mapsto\mathsf{bar}(a,x)])

\displaystyle{\cal E}\oplus\mathsf{bar}(a,\eta)

\displaystyle=(E_{e},\mathord{\twoheadrightarrow}\cup\left\{\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}(e^{\prime},e)\mid\\
\exists b\in R(\eta)\cup\{a\}:e^{\prime}=last_{\mathsf{bar}(b,\cdot)}({\cal E})\end{array}\right\},\Lambda,\ell[e\mapsto\mathsf{bar}(a,\eta)])

\displaystyle{\cal E}\oplus\mathsf{fnc}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=(E_{e},\mathord{\twoheadrightarrow}\cup\{(e^{\prime},e)\mid e^{\prime}\in last_{\mathit{Act}\setminus\{\mathsf{tst}_{\tau}(\cdot)\}}({\cal E})\},\Lambda,\ell[e\mapsto\mathsf{fnc}_{\tau}])

\displaystyle{\cal E}\oplus\mathsf{tst}_{\tau}(\beta)

\displaystyle=(E_{e},\mathord{\twoheadrightarrow}\cup\{(e^{\prime},e)\mid\exists a\in R(\beta).\ e^{\prime}\in last_{\mathsf{bar}(a,\cdot)}({\cal E})\},\Lambda,\ell[e\mapsto\mathsf{tst}_{\tau}(\beta)])

\displaystyle{\cal E}\oplus{\cal E}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[c]{@{}l}E\cup E^{\prime},\\
\twoheadrightarrow\cup\twoheadrightarrow^{\prime}\cup\left\{\begin{array}[c]{@{}l}(e,e^{\prime})\in E\times E^{\prime}\mid\exists x\in\textsc{Loc}.\\
e=last_{\{\mathsf{fnc},\mathsf{bar}(\cdot,\cdot),\mathsf{ff}_{\cdot}(x,\cdot)\}}({\cal E})\wedge\ell(e^{\prime})\in\mathit{Act}^{x}\end{array}\right\},\\
\Lambda\cup\Lambda^{\prime},\ell\cup\ell^{\prime}\end{array}\right)

Figure 6:
Operations for adding events to a conflict-free event
structure
{\cal E}=(E,\twoheadrightarrow,\Lambda,\ell)
, where
e\notin E
is a
fresh event and
E_{e}=E\cup\{e\}
,

{\cal E}^{\prime}=(E^{\prime},\twoheadrightarrow^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
,
E\cap E^{\prime}=\emptyset
)

The initial assertion in proof outlines is always the event structure
\mathsf{Ini}
.
Then, the proof rules successively add new events to the event structure when e.g. reading from or writing to shared variables. We however
never
add events for promises; rather, threads can first of all assume arbitrary promises of other threads having been made which they can read from.
The overall interference freedom constraint guarantees that these local assumptions about promises are met at the end.

For adding new events, we use a number of
\oplus
-operators, detailed in
Fig.

6
.
The event structures in there are local to threads and describe a single execution of the thread, hence are conflict-free.
The definition of these operators has to ensure that they capture the dependencies between views as defined by the operational semantics. For example, rule
Fulfill
requires (among others) the timestamp
t
to be larger than control view
v_{C}
, hence
{{\cal E}}\oplus\mathsf{ff}_{\tau}(x,\kappa)
has to introduce a flow from the last test event to the newly added fulfill event.

Figure 7:
Local proof rules for a thread
\tau

Figure

7
gives the proof rules for building local proof outlines of threads.
Most of the rules (i.e.,
PR-Write, PR-WriteR, PR-Fence, PR-Registers
and
PR-Assume
) just add one new event to the event structure recording the occurrence of a particular program statement. More complex are the two read rules:
PR-ReadEx
is applied for load statements reading from
x
when the event structure already contains an event
e
describing (in the sense of
\llbracket{\cal E}\rrbracket
) the entry in memory to read from; this can be a read, fulfill or the
\mathsf{ini}
event. In this case, the event structure after the load has to reflect the applicability condition of rule
Read
: no entries in memory to
x
in between
t
(the timestamp of
e
in
\llbracket{\cal E}\rrbracket
) and
v_{\mathit{read}}\sqcup coh(x)
. This is achieved by inserting an additional location restriction
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}{x}}
via the operator
\mathit{rstr}_{e}^{x}({\cal E})
to the following (potentially already
L
-labelled) flows (thus getting the restriction
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}L\cup\{x\}}
):

\{e\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}}}e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\in(\mathit{prFnc}_{\tau}({\cal E})\cap\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}({\cal E}))\cup\mathit{prBar}_{\tau}({\cal E})\cup\mathsf{Ff}_{\tau}^{x}({\cal E})\}\ .

Rule
PR-ReadNew
on the other hand introduces new read events into an event structure upon a load statement. The rule can directly introduce an entire
sequence
of read events (i.e., add a sequential event structure
{\cal E}^{\prime}
) as to enable later reads from memory entries which are prior to the entry of the current read (described by event
e
in the rule). This is required for message passing idioms like in the following program.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ \ }||@{\ \ }l}\begin{array}[t]{l}\textbf{Thread }1\\
1:\textnormal{{store}}\,\,x\,\,5;\\
2:\texttt{dmb};\\
3:\textnormal{{store}}\,\,y\,\,1;\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{l}\textbf{Thread }2\\
4:a:=\textnormal{{load}}\,y;\\
5:b:=\textnormal{{load}}\,x;\end{array}\end{array}

Here, due to the fence in Thread 1, Thread 2 – after having read
y
to be 1 – can only read
x
to be 5. When constructing the proof outline for Thread 2, we need to apply rule
PR-ReadNew
for the first load giving us

\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}{\mathsf{ini}\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{rd}_{1}(x,5)\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{rd}_{1}(y,1)\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{bar}(a,y)}

as assertion after statement 4. For the subsequent load we can then apply proof rule
PR-ReadEx
. Note that we could also construct a local proof outline having the load in line 4 read from
\mathsf{ini}
. This would then give us the two event structures of Example
1
which we, however, have already seen to not allow for an interference free configuration of their parallel composition.

Finally, we have a proof rule for parallel composition which combines local event structures when they are synchronisable and the resulting configuration is interference free.

\inference[\textsc{Parallel}]{\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.\ {\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}[\mathsf{Ini}]}\ S_{i}\ {\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}[{\cal E}_{i}]}\qquad\qquad{\cal E}_{1},\ldots,{\cal E}_{n}\text{ synchronisable }\\
\text{ interference free }C\in\mathit{Conf}({\cal E}_{1}||\ldots||{\cal E}_{n})}{{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}[\mathsf{Ini}]}\ S_{1}||\ldots||S_{n}\ {\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}[{\cal E}_{C}]}}

This rule ensures that (1) all synchronization constraints are met (i.e., the promises that threads want to read from have been made)
and (2) there is a configuration
C
of the combined event structure which is interference free.

without
a barrier in the writing thread. We see that here message passing is not

5.3
Soundness and Completeness

Due to lack of space, we can neither discuss soundness nor completeness of our proof calculus in some more detail here. Proofs can be found in the appendix.

Soundness requires proving all local proof rules correct plus showing the correctness of rule
Parallel
as of Theorem
5.1
below.
It states that whenever we find an interference free configuration in the parallel composition of synchronizable event structures in a locally sound proof outline, all thread states and memory contents matching this configuration are actually reachable by the concurrent program.

Theorem 5.1
()

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}[\mathsf{Ini}]}\ S_{i}\ {\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}[{\cal E}_{i}]}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, be proof outlines of threads
\tau_{1}
to
\tau_{n}
such that
{\cal E}_{1}
to
{\cal E}_{n}
are synchronizable and
let
\vec{T_{0}}
be an initial thread pool with
\vec{T_{0}}(\tau_{i})=(S_{i},ts_{\mathsf{ini}})
and
M_{0}=M_{\mathsf{ini}}
.

Then, for every thread pool
\vec{T}
with
\vec{T}(\tau_{i})=(\textnormal{{skip}},ts_{i})
, interference free configuration
C\in\mathit{Conf}({\cal E}_{1}||\ldots||{\cal E}_{n})
and memory
M
such that
\langle ts_{i},M\rangle\in\llbracket{\cal E}_{C}\rrbracket
,
tids(M)=\{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}\}
and
ts_{i}.prom=\emptyset
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we have
\langle\vec{T_{0}},M_{0}\rangle\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}^{*}\langle\vec{T},M\rangle
.

Our second main result is the
completeness
of the proof calculus: whenever there is an execution of a concurrent program, our proof calculus allows to show the reachability of its final state. More specifically, for every trace of a concurrent program we find local proof outlines with synchronizable event structures and an interference free configuration describing the final state of the trace.

Theorem 5.2
()

Let
\langle\vec{T_{0}},M_{0}\rangle\mathrel{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\mathrel{-}_{\vphantom{{}}}\mkern-8.0mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\cleaders\hbox{$\smash{\mkern-2.0mu\mathord{-}\mkern-2.0mu}$}\hfill\vphantom{\lower 1.0pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}}_{\vphantom{{}}}}}\mkern-11.0mu\rightarrow$}}^{*}\langle\vec{T},M\rangle
be a trace of a concurrent program over threads
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}
such that
\vec{T_{0}}
is the initial thread pool with
\vec{T_{0}}(\tau_{k})=(S_{k},ts_{\mathsf{ini}})
,
M_{0}=M_{\mathsf{ini}}
and

\vec{T}
the final thread pool with
\vec{T}(\tau_{k})=(\textnormal{{skip}},ts_{k})
and
ts_{k}.prom=\emptyset
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Then there are local proof outlines
{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}[\mathsf{Ini}]}\ S_{k}\ {\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}[{\cal E}_{k}]}
of threads
\tau_{k}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, such that
{\cal E}_{1}
to
{\cal E}_{n}
are synchronizable and there exists an interference free configuration
C\in\mathit{Conf}({\cal E}_{1}||\ldots||{\cal E}_{n})
with
\langle\vec{T},M\rangle\in\llbracket{\cal E}_{C}\rrbracket
.

6
Related Work

The first semantics of weak memory models employing
promises
has been proposed by Kang et al. in 2017
[
22
]
for building an operational semantics which allows modelling of read-write reordering while at the same time disallows out-of-thin-air behaviours. Our semantics here is a slightly simplified version of the promising semantics of ARMv8 given by Pulte et al.
[
31
]
. In particular, like
[
31
]
all program traces can be reordered so that the promise steps are all at the beginning which is a key property required for the soundness of our proof calculus.

There are already several proposals for program logics for weak memory
e.g.
[
13
,
38
,
10
,
34
,
15
,
23
,
1
,
14
,
11
]
.
The only one explicitly dealing with promises in the semantics is the
proposal of Svendsen et
al.
[
37
]
. They develop a safety proof calculus whereas we are interested in
reachability. Their logic furthermore has to deal with promises
occurring at any program step (as they show soundness with respect to
the promising semantics of
[
22
]
), whereas we rely on all
promises being made at the
beginning.

Partial order models of concurrency have already been used for giving
the semantics of memory
models
[
19
,
20
,
8
]
, but not
for reasoning. Wright et al
[
41
]
take
the approach of using a
semantic dependency
relation, which is
a partial order generated through an event structure representation
of a C/C++ program
[
30
]
, which is a
partial order over a thread’s execution. An Owicki-Gries logic is
provided to reason directly over such partial orders.
Incorrectness logic as used for proving reachability properties of
sequential
programs has been introduced by O’Hearn
[
28
]
, with a predecessor approach with (almost) the same principles by de Vries and Koutavas
[
39
]
. The first extension of incorrectness logic to concurrent programs has been proposed by Raad et al. in the form of an incorrectness separation logic
[
32
]
which is however not compositional.

Colvin
[
9
]
defines a semantics based
on a reordering relation for several hardware memory models, which
is then lifted to a Hoare calculus. This is then rephrased into a
reachability property by defining triples

\langle\!\langle p\rangle\!\rangle~{}s~{}\langle\!\langle q\rangle\!\rangle=\neg\{p\}~{}s~{}\{\neg q\}
, which states that it is
possible for
s
to reach
q
if execution starts in a state
satisfying
p
. Note that this is weaker than O’Hearn’s notion of
incompleteness, which states that all states satisfying
q
are reachable
from an execution starting in a state satisfying
p
.

7
Conclusion

In this paper, we have proposed a reachability (incorrectness) logic for concurrent programs running on weak memory models.
The reasoning technique is based on assertions which are event structures abstractly describing the contents of memory and the views of all threads. We have proven soundness and completeness of the proof calculus, and have demonstrated its applicability by proving the outcomes of some standard litmus tests to be reachable.

Acknowledgements.
We thank Christopher Pulte for clarifying one aspect of the Register rule of ARMv8’s operational semantics to us and Sadegh Dalvandi for initial discussions on the semantics.

Appendix 0.A
Further examples

RRC – Read Read Coherence

In the read read coherence litmus test the first two threads store different values to
x
, while the other two threads load
x
into different registers. The outcome
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\big{(}a=1\wedge b=2\wedge c=2\wedge d=1\big{)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\text{ \char 55}}}
should not be allowed and indeed, the parallel composition of local proof outlines does not give us an interference-free configuration.
Here, we also give the event names in the assertions.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ \ }||@{\ \ }l||@{\ \ }l||}\begin{array}[t]{l}\textbf{Thread }1\\

\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{l}\textbf{Thread }2\\

\end{array}\\

Here, the parallel composition of the local assertion event structures contains a cycle

(e_{\mathsf{ini}},e_{\mathsf{ini}},e_{\mathsf{ini}},e_{\mathsf{ini}})

(e_{1},*,e_{3},e_{9})
(*,*,e_{4},*)
(*,*,*,e_{10})
(*,e_{2},e_{5},e_{7})
(*,*,e_{6},*)

and therefore has no interference free configuration.

and appearing here in the form of

We see that due to the lack of a barrier in between statements 2 and 3 in Thread 2, the final outcome
{\color[rgb]{0.0,0.5,0.0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.0,0.5,0.0}\big{(}a=1\wedge b=1\wedge c=0\big{)}{\color[rgb]{0.0,0.5,0.0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.0,0.5,0.0}\text{\char 51}}}
is reachable.

SB – Store buffering

Next, we take a look at the standard SB example. We see that the outcome
{\color[rgb]{0.0,0.5,0.0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.0,0.5,0.0}\big{(}a=0\wedge b=0\big{)}{\color[rgb]{0.0,0.5,0.0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.0,0.5,0.0}\text{\char 51}}}
which is possible in lots of weak memory models is also possible here, and can be proven to be reachable.

{\color[rgb]{0.0,0.5,0.0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.0,0.5,0.0}\big{(}a=0\wedge b=0\big{)}{\color[rgb]{0.0,0.5,0.0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.0,0.5,0.0}\text{\char 51}}}

IRIW – Independent Reads of Independent Writes

In the IRIW litmus test we have two threads writing to shared variables
x
and
y
, respectively, and two other threads both reading both variables, but in a different order.
The outcome
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\big{(}a=1\wedge b=0\wedge c=1\wedge d=0\big{)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0,0}\text{ \char 55}}}
is not allowed by our semantics.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ \ }||@{\ \ }l||@{\ \ }l||@{\ \ }l}\begin{array}[t]{l}\textbf{Thread }1\\
{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}\big{[}\mathsf{ini}\big{]}}\\
\textnormal{{store}}\,\,x\,\,1;\\
{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}\big{[}\mathsf{ini}\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{ff}_{1}(x,1)\big{]}}\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{l}\textbf{Thread }2\\
{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}\big{[}\mathsf{ini}\big{]}}\\
a:=\textnormal{{load}}\,x;\\
{\color[rgb]{0.122,0.435,0.698}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0.122,0.435,0.698}\big{[}\mathsf{ini}\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{rd}_{1}(x,1)\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{bar}(a,x)\big{]}}\\
b:=\textnormal{{load}}\,y;\\
